\scriptstyle{\jmath_{a}}
\textstyle{\left(\dfrac{A}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\left\{\dfrac{A}{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus\{0+\mathfrak{p}\}^{-1}\right\}}

where
\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}
is given by
Theorem 24
, p. 96 of
[
6
]
.

Claim:
The following triangle commutes:

\textstyle{\dfrac{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{t_{\mathfrak{p}}}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
\textstyle{\left(\dfrac{A}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\left\{\dfrac{A}{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus\{0+\mathfrak{p}\}^{-1}\right\}}

Since
q_{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{p}}}
is an epimorphism, we have:

\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ t_{\mathfrak{p}}=\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ\overline{t_{\mathfrak{p}}}\iff(\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ t_{\mathfrak{p}})\circ q_{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{p}}}=(\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ\overline{t_{\mathfrak{p}}})\circ q_{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{p}}}

Now, by the universal property of
\eta_{A\setminus\mathfrak{p}}:A\to A_{\mathfrak{p}}
, there is
a unique

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
homomorphism
\xi:A_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\left(\dfrac{A}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\left\{\dfrac{A}{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus\{0+\mathfrak{p}\}^{-1}\right\}
such that:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\jmath_{a}\circ\eta_{b,\mathfrak{p}}\circ t_{a,b}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A\setminus\mathfrak{p}}}
\textstyle{A_{\mathfrak{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\left(\dfrac{A}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\left\{\dfrac{A}{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus\{0+\mathfrak{p}\}^{-1}\right\}}

commutes, so
(\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ t_{\mathfrak{p}})\circ q_{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{p}}}=\xi=(\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ\overline{t_{\mathfrak{p}}})\circ q_{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and the former triangle commutes.

Thus, since
\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}=\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ\overline{t_{\mathfrak{p}}}
where
\overline{t_{\mathfrak{p}}}
is an epimorphism which is also a section, it follows that
\overline{t_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
\Psi_{\mathfrak{p}}
are both
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
isomorphisms, as we wanted to show.

Remark 5

If
A
is a
von Neumann regular

C^{\infty}
-ring, then for each
a,b\in V
, let
e,f
(
ditto
) be the
unique
idempotents of
V
such that
(a)=(e)
and
(b)=(f)
. Then:

D^{\infty}(a)\cap Z^{\infty}(b)=D^{\infty}(e)\cap Z^{\infty}(f)=D^{\infty}(e)\cap D^{\infty}(1-f)=D^{\infty}(e.(1-f))

and

\left(\frac{A}{\sqrt[\infty]{(b)}}\right)\{(a+\sqrt[\infty]{(b)})^{-1}\}=\left(\frac{A}{\sqrt[\infty]{(f)}}\right)\{(e+\sqrt[\infty]{(f)})^{-1}\}=

=\left(\dfrac{A}{(f)}\right)\{(e+{(f)})^{-1}\}\cong A\{(1-f)^{-1}\}\{\eta_{1-f}(e)^{-1}\}\cong A\{((1-f)\cdot e)^{-1}\}

Thus the (pre)sheaf basis
F_{A}
is isomorphic to the “affine” sheaf basis
\Sigma_{A}
.

Now we proceed as follows:

\bullet
For each
C^{\infty}
-ring
A
,
F_{A}:(\mathcal{B}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)),\subseteq)^{\rm op}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf Rng}
is a presheaf on the (canonical) basis of the constructible topology in
{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A)
and with the stalks
\cong k_{\mathfrak{p}}(A)
,
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A)
. Moreover there is a canonical
C^{\infty}
-homomorphism
\phi_{A}:A\to F_{A}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)))\cong A/\sqrt[\infty]{(0)}
, in such a way that we obtain a functor
A\overset{\mathbb{F}}{\mapsto}F_{A}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)))
and a natural transformation
\phi:{\rm id}\Rightarrow\mathbb{F}
.

\bullet
For each
C^{\infty}
-ring
A
, take
G_{A}
the sheaf-basis functor associated to
F_{A}
- both are defined on the basis of the constructible topology of the
C^{\infty}
-spectrum of
A
and both share the same (up to isomorphisms) stalks. Moreover, since there is a natural transformation
F_{A}\to G_{A}
, there is a canonical
C^{\infty}
-homomorphism
\gamma_{A}:A\to G_{A}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)))
, in such a way that we obtain a functor
A\overset{\mathbb{G}}{\mapsto}G_{A}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)))
and a natural transformation
\gamma:id\to\mathbb{G}
.

\bullet
For each
C^{\infty}
-ring
A
, take
H_{A}
the (unique up to isomorphism) sheaf extension
G_{A}
to the basis of constructible topology into all the opens sets of this topology, thus
H_{A}\upharpoonright\cong G_{A}
, and both functor keeps the same stalks (up to isomorphism).
Moreover, since there is a natural transformation (isomorphism)
G_{A}\to H_{A}\upharpoonright
, there is a canonical
C^{\infty}
-homomorphism
\mu_{A}:A\to H_{A}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)))
, in such a way that we obtain a functor
A\overset{\mathbb{H}}{\mapsto}H_{A}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)))
and a natural transformation
\mu:{\rm id}\Rightarrow\mathbb{H}
.

\bullet
Since
H_{A}
is a sheaf of
C^{\infty}
-rings, defined over all the opens of a boolean space, and whose stalks are
C^{\infty}
-fields, then
\mathbb{H}(A)=H_{A}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)))
is a Von Neumann regular
C^{\infty}
-ring. Thus
A\overset{\mathbb{H}}{\mapsto}H_{A}({\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}\,(A)))
determines a functor
C^{\infty}{\rm\bf Rng}\to C^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}
and we have a natural transformation
\mu:{\rm id}\Rightarrow i\circ\mathbb{H}
.

We are ready to (re)state the:

Theorem 2.6

(bis)
The inclusion functor
C^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}{\rm\bf Rng}
has a left adjoint functor
{\rm vN}:C^{\infty}{\rm\bf Rng}\to C^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}
. In more details: for
A
be any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring, the pair
(\mathbb{H}(A)),\mu_{A}:A\to\mathbb{H}(A)))
is the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
von Neumann regular hull of
A
, that is, for every von Neumann-regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring
V
and for every
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
homomorphism
f:A\to V
there is a unique
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
homomorphism
\widetilde{f}:A_{\omega}\rightarrow V
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\Sigma(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\widetilde{f}}
\textstyle{V}

Proof

We just have observed above that all the conditions in the
Theorem
2.4
hold.

We finish this subsection with some general applications of the adjunction above and one of its specific “sheaf-theorectic” construction of the left adjoint
{\rm vN}:C^{\infty}{\rm\bf Rng}\to C^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}
.

Proposition 8

The functor
{\rm vN}
preserves all colimits. In particular it preserves:

•

directed inductive limits;

•

coproducts (=
\infty-
tensor products in
{\rm\bf C^{\infty}-Rng}
);

•

coequalizers/quotients.

Proof

Since it is a left adjoint,
{\rm vN}
preserves all colimits. We explain the meaning of the preservation of quotients. Consider the induced homomorphism
vN(q_{I}):{\rm vN}(A)\rightarrow{\rm vN}(A/I)
: it is surjective since the coequalizers in
{\rm\bf C^{\infty}-Rng}
and
{\rm\bf C^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}}
coincide with the surjective homomorphisms. Then
\bar{I}:=\ker({\rm vN}(q_{I}))\subseteq{\rm vN}(A)
is such that
\overline{{\rm vN}(q_{I})}:{\rm vN}(A)/\bar{I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm vN}(A/I)
and, since

{\rm vN}(A)\rightarrowtail\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spec^{\infty}}(A)}k_{\mathfrak{p}}(A),

\bar{I}
can be identified with
{\rm vN}(A)\cap\{\vec{x}=(x_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spec^{\infty}}(A)}\in\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spec^{\infty}}(A)}k_{\mathfrak{p}}(A):\forall\mathfrak{p}\supseteq I,x_{\mathfrak{p}}=0\in k_{\mathfrak{p}}(A)\}
. Note in particular, that if
I\subseteq\sqrt[\infty]{(0)}
, then
{\rm vN}(q_{I}):{\rm vN}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm vN}(A/I)
, thus
\bar{I}=\{0\}
.

The following results are specific to the functor
{\rm vN}
, i.e., they are not general consequences of it being a left adjoint functor.

Proposition 9

{\rm vN}
preserves localizations. More precisely, given a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-ring
A
and a subset
S\subseteq A
, denote
S^{\prime}:=\eta_{A}[S]\subseteq{\rm vN}(A)
the corresponding subset and let
\eta_{A}^{S}:A\{S^{-1}\}\rightarrow{\rm vN}(A)\{{S^{\prime}}^{-1}\}
be the induced arrow, i.e.,
\eta_{A}^{S}
is the unique homomorphism such that
\eta_{A}^{S}\circ\sigma(A)_{S}=\sigma({\rm vN}(A))_{S^{\prime}}\circ\eta_{A}
. Then
\eta_{A\{S^{-1}\}}:A\{S^{-1}\}\rightarrow{\rm vN}(A\{S\}^{-1})
thus it is isomorphic to the arrow
\eta_{A}^{S}
, through the obvious pair of inverse (iso)morphisms
{\rm vN}(A\{S\}^{-1})\rightleftarrows{\rm vN}(A)\{S^{\prime}\}^{-1}
.

Proof

First of all, note that
{\rm vN}(A)\{S^{\prime}\}^{-1}
is a vN-regular ring, cf.
Proposition
1
.
For each vN-regular ring
V
, the bijection
(-\circ\eta_{A}):{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}}({\rm vN}(A),V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-Rng}(A,V)
restricts to the bijection:

(-\circ\eta_{A}){\upharpoonright}:\{H\in{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}}({\rm vN}(A),V):H[S^{\prime}]\subseteq V^{\times}\}

.
\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}\{h\in{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-Rng}(A,V):h[S]\subseteq V^{\times}\}.

Composing the last bijection with the bijections below, obtained from the universal property of localizations,

(-\circ\sigma(A)_{S})^{-1}:\{h\in{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-Rng}(A,V):h[S]\subseteq V^{\times}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-Rng}(A[S]^{-1},V),

(-\circ\sigma({\rm vN}(A))_{S^{\prime}}):{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-Rng}({\rm vN}(A)\{S^{\prime-1}\},V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}\{H\in{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-Rng}({\rm vN}(A),V):H[S^{\prime}]\subseteq V^{\times}\}

we obtain, since
{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}}
is a full subcategory of
{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}Rng}
, the bijection

(-\circ\eta^{S}_{A}):{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}}({\rm vN}(A)\{S^{\prime-1}\},V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm\bf\mathcal{C}^{\infty}Rng}(A\{S^{-1}\},V).

Summing up, the arrow
\eta_{A}^{S}:A\{S^{-1}\}\rightarrow{\rm vN}(A)\{S^{\prime-1}\}
satisfies the universal property of vN-regular hull, thus it is isomorphic to the arrow
\eta_{A\{S^{-1}\}}:A\{S^{-1}\}\rightarrow{\rm vN}(A\{S^{-1}\})
.

The following result shows us how useful is the sheaf-theoretic description of the vN-hull of a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-ring.

Proposition 10

{\rm vN}
preserves finite products.
More precisely, let
I
be a finite set and
\{A_{i}:i\in I\}
any family of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-rings. Denote
\pi_{j}:\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A_{j}
the projection homomorphism,
j\in I
. Then
\prod_{i\in I}\eta_{A_{i}}:\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}{\rm vN}(A_{i})
satisfies the universal property of vN-regular hull, thus it is isomorphic to the arrow
\eta_{\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}}:\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}\rightarrow{\rm vN}(\prod_{i\in I}A_{i})
, through the obvious pair of inverse (iso)morphisms
{\rm vN}(\prod_{i\in I}A_{i})\rightleftarrows\prod_{i\in I}{\rm vN}(A_{i})
.

Proof

(Sketch) First of all, note that
\prod_{i\in I}{\rm vN}(A_{i})
is a vN-regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-ring. If
I=\emptyset
, then
\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
,
\prod_{i\in I}{\rm vN}(A_{i})
and
{\rm vN}(\prod_{i\in I}A_{i})
are isomorphic to the trivial
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-ring
\{0\}
, thus the result holds in this case.

By induction, we only need to see that
({\rm vN}(\pi_{1}),{\rm vN}(\pi_{2})):{\rm vN}(A_{1}\times A_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm vN}(A_{1})\times{\rm vN}(A_{2})
. But we have:

\bullet

[\pi_{1}^{\star},\pi_{2}^{\star}]:{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{1})\sqcup{\rm Spec^{\infty}}(A_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\approx}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{1}\times A_{2})
,
(p_{i},i)\mapsto\pi_{i}^{-1}[p_{i}]
,
i=1,2
.

\bullet

[\pi_{1}^{\star},\pi_{2}^{\star}]:{\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}(A_{1})\sqcup{\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}(A_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\approx}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm Spec}^{\infty-{\rm const}}(A_{1}\times A_{2})
.

\bullet
Let
a:=(a_{1},a_{2})\in A_{1}\times A_{2}
and
\bar{b}:=\{(b_{1,1},b_{2,1}),\cdots,(b_{1,n},b_{2,n})\}\subseteq_{{\rm fin}}A_{1}\times A_{2}
, then
(A_{1}\times A_{2})_{a,\bar{b}}\ \cong\ {A_{1}}_{a_{1},\bar{b}_{1}}\times{A_{2}}_{a_{2},\bar{b}_{2}}
.

\bullet

\sqcup_{p\in{\rm Spe\mathcal{C}^{\infty}}(A_{1}\times A_{2})}k_{\mathfrak{p}}(A_{1}\times A_{2})\approx(\sqcup_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}\in{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{1})}k_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}(A_{1}))\sqcup(\sqcup_{\mathfrak{p}_{2}\in{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{1})}k_{\mathfrak{p}_{2}}(A_{2}))
.

\bullet
Let
U\in{\rm Open}({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{1}\times A_{2}))
, then
U\approx U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}
, where
U_{i}=(\pi_{i}^{\star})^{-1}[U]\in{\rm Open}({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{i}))
. Then any sheaf
S
satisfies
S(A)(U)\ \cong\ S(A_{1})(U_{1})\times S(A_{2})(U_{2})
.

Now the construction of
{\rm vN}
as the global sections of of a sheaf allows to conclude
{\rm vN}(A_{1}\times A_{2})=S(A_{1}\times A_{2})({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{1}\times A_{2}))\ \cong\ (S(A_{1})({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{1}))\times S(A_{2})({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A_{2})))={\rm vN}(A_{1})\times{\rm vN}(A_{2})
.

3
Von Neumann-regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
Rings and Boolean Algebras

In this section we also apply von Neumann regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-ring to naturally represent Boolean Algebras in a strong sense: i.e., not only all Boolean algebras are isomorphic to the Boolean algebra of idempotents of a von Neumann regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-ring, as every homomorphism between such boolean algebras of idempotents is induced by a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-homomorphism.

We start with the following general results:

Proposition 11

Let
(L,\wedge,\vee,\leq)
be any lattice. Then:

(\forall a\in L)(\forall b\in L)(\forall c\in L)(a\wedge(b\vee c)=(a\wedge b)\vee(a\wedge c))

if, and only if,

(\forall a\in L)(\forall b\in L)(\forall c\in L)(a\vee(b\wedge c)=(a\vee b)\wedge(a\vee c))

Proof

See, for example,
Lemma 6.3
of
[
11
]
.

Proposition 12

Let
(A,+,\cdot,0,1)
be any commutative unital ring and define
B(A):=\{e\in A|e^{2}=e\}
. Then
(B(A),\wedge,\vee,*,\leq,\bot,\top)
, where:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\wedge:&B(A)\times B(A)&\rightarrow&B(A)\\
&(e,e^{\prime})&\mapsto&e\cdot e^{\prime}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vee:&B(A)\times B(A)&\rightarrow&B(A)\\
&(e,e^{\prime})&\mapsto&e+e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{cccc}*:&B(A)&\rightarrow&B(A)\\
&e&\mapsto&1-e\end{array}

\leq=\{(e,e^{\prime})\in B(A)\times B(A)|e\cdot e^{\prime}=e\}

\top=1\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\bot=0

is a bounded, distributive and complemented lattice, i.e., a Boolean Algebra.

Proof

Claim:

(e\wedge e^{\prime})^{2}=e\wedge e^{\prime}
, so
e\wedge e^{\prime}\in B(A)
.

(e\wedge e^{\prime})^{2}=(e\cdot e^{\prime})^{2}=e\cdot e^{\prime}\cdot e\cdot e^{\prime}=e^{2}\cdot{e^{\prime}}^{2}=e\cdot e^{\prime}=e\wedge e^{\prime}
.

Claim:

(e\vee e^{\prime})^{2}=e\vee e^{\prime}
, so
e\vee e^{\prime}\in B(A)
.

(e\vee e^{\prime})^{2}=(e+e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime})^{2}=e^{2}+2\cdot e\cdot e^{\prime}+{e^{\prime}}^{2}-2(e^{2}\cdot e^{\prime}+e\cdot{e^{\prime}}^{2})+{e\cdot e^{\prime}}^{2}=\\
=e^{2}+{e^{\prime}}^{2}+2\cdot e\cdot e^{\prime}-2\cdot e\cdot e^{\prime}-2\cdot e^{2}\cdot e^{\prime}-2\cdot e\cdot{e^{\prime}}^{2}+e\cdot e^{\prime}=e^{2}+{e^{\prime}}^{2}-e\cdot e^{\prime}=e+e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime}=e\vee e^{\prime}

Claim
: For any
e\in B(A)
,
e^{*}=1-e\in B(A)
.

(1-e)^{2}=1-2\cdot e+e^{2}=1-e.

Note that for any
e\in B(A)
,
e\vee e^{*}=e\vee(1-e)=e+(1-e)-e\cdot(1-e)=1
.

Claim:
Given any
e,e^{\prime}\in B(A)
,
e\vee e^{\prime}=\sup\{e,e^{\prime}\}
.

We have:

e\cdot(e\vee e^{\prime})=e\cdot(e+e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime})=e^{2}+e\cdot e^{\prime}-e^{2}\cdot e^{\prime}=e+e\cdot e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime}=e,\,\,\mbox{so}\,\,e\leq(e\vee e^{\prime}).

A similar reasoning shows us that
e^{\prime}\leq(e\vee e^{\prime})
.

Let
f\in B(A)
be such that
e\leq f
and
e^{\prime}\leq f
. Then:

e\cdot f=e\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,e^{\prime}\cdot f=e^{\prime},

so

f\cdot(e\vee e^{\prime})=f\cdot(e+e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime})=f\cdot e+f\cdot e^{\prime}-f\cdot e\cdot e^{\prime}=e+e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime}=e\vee e^{\prime},

hence
e\vee e^{\prime}=\sup\{e,e^{\prime}\}
.

Claim:
Given any
e,e^{\prime}\in B(A)
,
e\wedge e^{\prime}=\inf\{e,e^{\prime}\}
.

We have:

e\cdot e^{\prime}=e^{2}\cdot e^{\prime}=e\cdot(e\cdot e^{\prime})=e\cdot(e\wedge e^{\prime}),\,\,\,\mbox{so}\,\,e\wedge e^{\prime}\leq e.

A similar reasoning shows us that
e\wedge e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime}
.

Let
f\in B(A)
be such that
f\leq e
and
f\leq e^{\prime}
, so
f\cdot e=f
and
f\cdot e^{\prime}=f
.

We have:

f\cdot(e\wedge e^{\prime})=f\cdot(e\cdot e^{\prime})=(f\cdot e)\cdot e^{\prime}=f\cdot e^{\prime}=f,\,\,\,\mbox{so}\,\,f\leq e\wedge e^{\prime},

hence
e\wedge e^{\prime}=\inf\{e,e^{\prime}\}
.

Note that since
(\forall e\in B(A))(e\cdot 0=0)
, then
(\forall e\in B(A))(0\leq e)
so
0=\bot
, and since
(\forall e\in B(A))(e\cdot 1=e)
, then
(\forall e\in B(A))(e\leq 1)
, so
1=\top
.

Claim:

(\forall e\in B(A))(\forall f\in B(A))(\forall g\in B(A))(e\wedge(f\vee g)=(e\wedge f)\vee(e\wedge g)).

e\wedge(f\vee g)=e\cdot(f\vee g)=e\cdot(f+g-f\cdot g)=e\cdot f+e\cdot g-e\cdot f\cdot g=\\
=(e\cdot f)\vee(e\cdot g)=e\cdot f+e\cdot g-(e\cdot f)\cdot(e\cdot g)=e\cdot f+e\cdot g-e\cdot f\cdot g=e\cdot(f+g-f\cdot g)=e\wedge(f\vee g)

By
Proposition
11
, it follows that:

(\forall e\in B(A))(\forall f\in B(A))(\forall g\in B(A))(e\vee(f\wedge g)=(e\vee f)\wedge(e\vee g)).

It follows from these considerations that
(B(A),\wedge,\vee,*,\leq,\bot,\top)
is a bounded, distributive and complemented lattice, hence a Boolean algebra.

Remark 6

By Stone Duality, there is an anti-equivalence of categories between the category of Boolean algebras,
{\rm\bf Bool}
, and the category of Boolean spaces,
{\rm\bf BoolSp}
.

Under this anti-equivalence, a Boolean space
(X,\tau)
is mapped to the Boolean algebra of clopen subsets of
(X,\tau)
,
{\rm Clopen}\,(X)
:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}{\rm Clopen}:&{\rm\bf BoolSp}&\rightarrow&{\rm\bf Bool}\\
&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.5538pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.5538pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(X,\tau)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.00922pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.5538pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.5538pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(Y,\sigma)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}&\mapsto&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 27.1528pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-27.1528pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm Clopen}\,(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.21349pt\raise 6.80057pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f^{-1}\upharpoonright}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.1528pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 51.1528pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm Clopen}\,(X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

The quasi-inverse functor is given by the Stone space functor: a Boolean algebra
B
is mapped to the Stone space of
B
,
{\rm Stone}(B)=(\{U\subseteq B:U\,\,\text{is an ultrafilter in}\,\,B\},\tau_{B})
, where
\tau_{B}
is the topology whose basis is given by the image of the map
t_{B}:B\to\wp({\rm Stone}(B))
,
b\mapsto t_{B}(b)=S_{B}(b)=\{U\in{\rm Stone}(B):b\in U\}
.

\begin{array}[]{cccc}{\rm Stone}:&{\rm\bf Bool}&\rightarrow&{\rm\bf BoolSp}\\
&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.0434pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.0434pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.40392pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{h}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.0434pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.0434pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}&\mapsto&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.55733pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.55733pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{{\rm Stone}(B^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.65738pt\raise 6.78487pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.45517pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{h^{-1}\upharpoonright}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.55733pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.55733pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm Stone}(B)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Remark 7

Let
(A^{\prime},+^{\prime},\cdot^{\prime},0^{\prime},1^{\prime})
be any commutative unital ring and denote by
\wedge^{\prime},\vee^{\prime},*^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},0^{\prime}
and
1^{\prime}
its respective associated Boolean algebra operations, relations and constant symbols as constructed above. Note that for any commutative unital ring homomorphism
f:A\to A^{\prime}
, the map
B(f):=f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}:B(A)\to A^{\prime}
is such that:

(i)

B(f)[B(A)]\subseteq B(A^{\prime})
;

(ii)

(\forall e_{1}\in A)(\forall e_{2}\in A)(B(f)(e_{1}\wedge e_{2})=f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(e_{1}\cdot e_{2})=(f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(e_{1}))\cdot^{\prime}(f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(e_{2}))=B(f)(e_{1})\wedge^{\prime}B(f)(e_{2}))

(iii)

(\forall e_{1}\in A)(\forall e_{2}\in A)(B(f)(e_{1}\vee e_{2})=f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(e_{1}+e_{2})=(f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(e_{1}))+^{\prime}(f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(e_{2}))=B(f)(e_{1})\vee^{\prime}B(f)(e_{2}))

(iv)

(\forall e\in B(A))(B(f)(e^{*})=f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(1-e)=f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(1)-f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(e)=1^{\prime}-f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}(e)=B(f)(e)^{*})

hence a morphism of Boolean algebras.

We also have, for every ring
A
,
B({\rm id}_{A})={\rm id}_{B(A)}
and given any
f:A\to A^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime\prime}
,
B(f^{\prime}\circ f)=B(f^{\prime})\circ B(f)
, since
B(f)=f\upharpoonright_{B(A)}
, so:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}B:&{\rm\bf CRing}&\rightarrow&{\rm\bf Bool}\\
&A&\mapsto&B(A)\\
&{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.10934pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}&\mapsto&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.68231pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.68231pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.44563pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{B(f)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.68231pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.68231pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B(A^{\prime})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

is a (covariant) functor.

Since we can regard any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring
A
as a commutative unital ring via the forgetful functor
\widetilde{U}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf Rng}\to{\rm\bf CRing}
, we have a (covariant) functor:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\widetilde{B}:&\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf Rng}&\rightarrow&{\rm\bf Bool}\\
&A&\mapsto&\widetilde{B}(A):=(B\circ U)(A)\\
&{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.10934pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}&\mapsto&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 13.41669pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.41669pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{B}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.59372pt\raise 7.48611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.73611pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\widetilde{B}(f)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.41669pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 37.41669pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{B}(A^{\prime})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Now, if
A
is any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring, we can define the following map:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\jmath_{A}:&\widetilde{B}(A)&\rightarrow&{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A))\\
&e&\mapsto&D^{\infty}(e)=\{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)|e\notin\mathfrak{p}\}\end{array}

Claim:
The map defined above is a Boolean algebra homomorphism.

Note that for any
e\in B(A)
,
D^{\infty}(e^{*})=D^{\infty}(1-e)={\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)\setminus D^{\infty}(e)=D^{\infty}(e)^{*}
, since:

D^{\infty}(e)\cap D^{\infty}(1-e)=D^{\infty}\,(e\cdot(1-e))=D^{\infty}(0)=\varnothing

and

D^{\infty}(e)\cup D^{\infty}(e*)=D^{\infty}(e)\cup D^{\infty}(1-e)=D^{\infty}(e^{2}+(1-e)^{2})=D^{\infty}(e+(1-e))=\\
=D^{\infty}(1)={\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A).

Hence
\jmath_{A}(e^{*})=\jmath_{A}(1-e)=D^{\infty}(1-e)={\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)\setminus D^{\infty}\,(e)={D^{\infty}(e)}^{*}=\jmath_{A}(e)^{*}
.

By the item (iii) of
Lemma 1.4
of
[
24
]
,
D^{\infty}(e\cdot e^{\prime})=D^{\infty}(e)\cap D^{\infty}(e^{\prime})
, so:

\jmath_{A}(e\wedge e^{\prime})=D^{\infty}(e\cdot e^{\prime})=D^{\infty}(e)\cap D^{\infty}(e^{\prime})=\jmath_{A}(e)\cap\jmath_{A}(e^{\prime}).

Last,

\jmath_{A}(e\vee e^{\prime})=\jmath_{A}(e+e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime})=D^{\infty}(e+e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime})=D^{\infty}(e^{2})\cup D^{\infty}(e^{\prime}-e\cdot e^{\prime})=\\
D^{\infty}(e)\cup D^{\infty}(e^{\prime}\cdot(1-e))=D^{\infty}(e)\cup[D^{\infty}(e^{\prime})\cap D^{\infty}(1-e)]=\\
=[D^{\infty}(e)\cup D^{\infty}(e^{\prime})]\cap[D^{\infty}(e)\cup D^{\infty}(1-e)]=[D^{\infty}(e)\cup D^{\infty}(e^{\prime})]\cap{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)=\\
=D^{\infty}(e)\cup D^{\infty}(e^{\prime})=\jmath_{A}(e)\cup\jmath_{A}(e^{\prime}),

and the claim is proved.

The map
\jmath_{A}:B(A)\to{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)
suggests that the idempotent elements of the Boolean algebra
B(A)
associated with a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring
A
hold a strong relationship with the canonical basis of the Zariski topology of
{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A)
. In this section we are going to show that these idempotent elements, in the case of the von Neumann-regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
rings, represent
all the Boolean algebras
.

Theorem 3.1

Let
A
be a von Neumann regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring. The map:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\jmath_{A}:&\widetilde{B}(A)&\rightarrow&{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A))\\
&e&\mapsto&D^{\infty}(e)=\{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)|e\notin\mathfrak{p}\}\end{array}

is an isomorphism of Boolean algebras.

Proof

It has already been proved that
\jmath_{A}:B(A)\to{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)
is a homomorphism of Boolean algebras, so in order to prove that
\jmath_{A}:\widetilde{B}(A)\to{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A))
is an injective map, it suffices to show that:

\jmath_{A}^{\dashv}[{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)]=\{1\}.

(see
Lemma 4.8
of
[
2
]
)

Claim:

(\jmath_{A}(e)={\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A))\iff(e\in A^{\times})
.

Indeed,

a\in A^{\times}\Rightarrow D^{\infty}(a)={\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A).

On the other hand, if
a\notin A^{\times}
then there is some maximal
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
radical prime ideal
\mathfrak{m}\in{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)
such that
a\in\mathfrak{m}
. If
\mathfrak{m}\notin D^{\infty}(a)
then
\jmath_{A}(a)=D^{\infty}(a)\neq{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)
. Hence
\jmath_{A}(a)={\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)\Rightarrow a\in A^{\times}
.

Let
e\in\widetilde{B}(A)
be such that
\jmath_{A}(e)=D^{\infty}(e)={\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)
, so
(\forall\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A))(e\notin\mathfrak{p})
.

Since
0\in\mathfrak{p}
for every
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)
, then
(e)\neq(0)
, so
(e)=A=(1)
, and
e\in A^{\times}
and since
e
is idempotent,
e=1
.

We already know that
\jmath_{A}
is an injective Boolean algebras homomorphism, so it suffices to prove that it is also surjective.

Claim:
Given any
a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{n}\in A
, there is some
b\in A
such that:

D^{\infty}(a_{1})\cup D^{\infty}(a_{2})\cup\cdots\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n})=D^{\infty}(b).

We are going to prove this claim by induction.

Case 1:

n=2
.

By the item (iii) of
Lemma 1.4
of
[
24
]
, we know that for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
,

D^{\infty}(a_{1})\cup D^{\infty}(a_{2})=D^{\infty}(a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}),

so we can take
b=a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}
.

Inductive step:
Suppose that given
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n-1}\in A
there is some
b_{n-1}\in A
such that:

D^{\infty}(a_{1})\cup\cdots\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n-1})=D^{\infty}(b_{n-1}).

Given
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
, we know by the inductive step, that there is some
b_{n-1}\in A
such that:

D^{\infty}(a_{1})\cup\cdots\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n-1})=D^{\infty}(b_{n-1}).

Considering
b={b_{n-1}}^{2}+a_{n}^{2}
, we have:

D^{\infty}(a_{1})\cup\cdots\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n-1})\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n})=D^{\infty}(b_{n-1})\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n})=D^{\infty}(b),

which proves the claim.

Let
E\in{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A))
. Since
\{D^{\infty}(a)|a\in A\}
is a basis for the topology of the compact topological space
{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A)
, there are
a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{n}\in A
such that:

E=D^{\infty}(a_{1})\cup D^{\infty}(a_{2})\cup\cdots\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n}).

By the above claim, there is some
b\in A
such that
E=D^{\infty}(a_{1})\cup D^{\infty}(a_{2})\cup\cdots\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n})=D^{\infty}(b)
.

Now, since
A
is a von Neumann regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring, there is some idempotent element
e\in A
such that
(b)=(e)
.

We claim that
\jmath_{A}(e)=E
.

We have
\jmath_{A}(e):=D^{\infty}(e)=D^{\infty}(b)=D^{\infty}(a_{1})\cup\cdots\cup D^{\infty}(a_{n})=E
, and it follows that
\jmath_{A}:\widetilde{B}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A))
is an isomorphism.

Theorem 3.2

We have the following diagram of categories, functors and natural isomorphisms:

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{B}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Spec}^{\infty}}
\textstyle{{\rm\bf BoolSp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Clopen}}
\scriptstyle{\jmath}
\textstyle{{\rm\bf Bool}}

Proof

First note that since
A
is a von Neumann-regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring, the set of the compact open subsets of (the Boolean space)
{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A)
equals
{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A))
.

On the one hand, given a von Neumann regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring
A
, we have:

({\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}))(A)={\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A))=\{D^{\infty}(e)|e\in\widetilde{B}(A)\}

For every von Neumann regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring
A
, by
Theorem
3.1
, we have the following isomorphism of Boolean algebras:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\jmath_{A}:&\widetilde{B}(A)&\rightarrow&{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(A))\\
&e&\mapsto&D^{\infty}(e)\end{array}

It is easy to see that for every
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
homomorphism
f:A\to A^{\prime}
, we have the following commutative rectangle:

\textstyle{\widetilde{B}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{B}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\jmath_{A}}
\textstyle{{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(f))}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}(A^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\jmath_{A^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\rm Clopen}\,({\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(A^{\prime}))}

In fact, given
e\in\widetilde{B}(A)
, we have, on the one hand,
\jmath_{A}(e)=D_{A}^{\infty}(e)
and
{\rm Clopen}({\rm Spec}^{\infty}(f))(D_{A}^{\infty}(e))=D_{A^{\prime}}^{\infty}(f(e))
. On the other hand,
\jmath_{A^{\prime}}\circ\widetilde{B}(f)(e)=\jmath_{A^{\prime}}(D_{A^{\prime}}^{\infty}(f(e)))
, so the rectangle commutes.

Thus
\jmath
is a natural transformation. Thus
\textstyle{\jmath:\widetilde{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Clopen}\circ{\rm Spec}^{\infty}}
is a natural isomorphism and the diagram “commutes” (up to natural isomorphism).

Lemma 6

Let
(X,\tau)
be a Boolean topological space, and let:

\mathcal{R}=\left\{R\subseteq X\times X|(R\,\,{\rm is\,\,an\,\,equivalence\,\,relation\,\,on}\,X)\&(\dfrac{X}{R}\,{\rm is\,\,a\,\,discrete\,\,compact\,\,space})\right\},

which is partially ordered by inclusion. Whenever
R_{i},R_{j}\in\mathcal{R}
are such that
R_{j}\subseteq R_{i}
, we have the continuous surjective map:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mu_{R_{j}R_{i}}:&\dfrac{X}{R_{j}}&\twoheadrightarrow&\dfrac{X}{R_{i}}\\
&[x]_{R_{j}}&\mapsto&[x]_{R_{j}}\end{array}

so we have the inverse system
\{\frac{X}{R_{i}};\mu_{R_{j}R_{i}}:\frac{X}{R_{j}}\to\frac{X}{R_{i}}\}
. By definition (see, for instance,
[
13
]
),

\varprojlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{X}{R}=\left\{([x]_{R_{i}})_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}\in\prod_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{X}{R}|(R_{j}\subseteq R_{i}\to(p_{R_{i}}(([x]_{R_{i}})_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}})=\mu_{R_{j}R_{i}}\circ p_{R_{j}}(([x]_{R_{i}})_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}})))\right\}

Let
X_{\infty}
denote
\varprojlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{X}{R}
, so we have the following cone:

\textstyle{X_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{i}}}
\textstyle{\frac{X}{R_{j}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{j}R_{i}}}
\textstyle{\frac{X}{R_{i}}}

We consider
X_{\infty}
together with the induced subspace topology of
\prod_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{X}{R}
.

By the universal property of
X_{\infty}
, there is a unique continuous map map
\delta:X\to X_{\infty}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{R_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{q_{R_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\delta}
\textstyle{X_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{i}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{R_{j}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{j}R_{i}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{R_{i}}}

We claim that such a
\delta:X\to X_{\infty}
is a homeomorphism, so:

X\cong\varprojlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{X}{R}

that is,
X
a profinite space.

Proof

First we observe that
\mathcal{R}
is a downwards directed set.

Claim:
Given any
R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathcal{R}
,
R_{1}\cap R_{2}\in\mathcal{R}
.

Naturally, if both
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are equivalence relations on
X
, then
R_{1}\cap R_{2}
is also an equivalence relation on
X
, and it is such that
R_{1}\cap R_{2}\subseteq R_{1}
and
R_{1}\cap R_{2}\subseteq R_{2}
. Moreover, we have an injective continuous map:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\varphi:&\dfrac{X}{R_{1}\cap R_{2}}&\rightarrowtail&\dfrac{X}{R_{1}}\times\dfrac{X}{R_{2}}\\
&[x]_{R_{1}\cap R_{2}}&\mapsto&([x]_{R_{1}},[x]_{R_{2}})\end{array}

Claim:
Given
R\in\mathcal{R}
, if
S
is any equivalence relation on
X
such that
R\subseteq S
, then
S\in\mathcal{R}
.

We know that each
\frac{X}{R}
is a compact, Hausdorff and totally disconnected space, hence
\prod_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{X}{R}
is also a compact, Hausdorff and totally disconnected space,
i.e.
, a Boolean space.

By
Theorem 2.4
of
[
13
]
, since each space
\frac{X}{R_{i}}
is a Hausdorff and compact space,
X_{\infty}
is also a Hausdorff and compact space, so it is closed in the Boolean space
\prod_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{X}{R}
, hence it is a Boolean space.

Consider:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\delta_{X}:&X&\rightarrow&X_{\infty}\\
&x&\mapsto&([x]_{R_{i}})_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}\end{array}

Claim 1:

\delta_{X}
is injective;

Let
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,
x\neq x^{\prime}
. Since
(X,\tau)
is a Boolean space, there exists some clopen
U\in\tau
such that
x\in U
and
x^{\prime}\notin U
, that is,
x^{\prime}\notin U
.

Consider the following equivalence relation on
X
:

R=(U\times U)\cup(X\setminus U\times X\setminus U)

and note that
\frac{X}{R}
is a discrete topological space, hence
R\in\mathcal{R}
. We have
(x,x^{\prime})\notin R
, so for every
S\in\mathcal{R}
such that
R\supseteq S
we have
[x]_{S}\neq[x^{\prime}]_{S}
, so
\delta_{X}(x)\neq\delta_{X}(x^{\prime})
.

Claim 2:

{\rm Cl}.(\delta_{X}[X])=X_{\infty}
.

Let
\Omega=\left(\{[a_{1}]_{R_{1}}\}\times\{[a_{2}]_{R_{2}}\}\times\cdots\times\{[a_{n}]_{R_{n}}\}\times\prod_{R\neq R_{1},\cdots,R_{n}}\frac{X}{R}\right)\cap X_{\infty}\subset X_{\infty}

be a basic open set of
X_{\infty}
. Let:

R^{\prime}=R_{1}\cap R_{2}\cap\cdots\cap R_{n}\in\mathcal{R}.

Since
R_{i}\subseteq R_{j}
implies that:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{R_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{q_{R_{j}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{R_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{i}R_{j}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{R_{j}}}

commutes, the map:

\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{R_{1}\cap R_{2}\cap\cdots\cap R_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{R_{1}}\times\dfrac{X}{R_{2}}\times\cdots\times\dfrac{X}{R_{n}}}

is injective.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is a directed set, we can assume without lost of generality, that
\{R_{1},R_{2},\cdots,R_{n}\}
has a minimum, that we shall denote by
R^{\prime}=\min\{R_{1},\cdots,R_{n}\}
.

Now we can chose any
[a^{\prime}]_{R^{\prime}}\in\frac{X}{R^{\prime}}
. We claim that
\delta_{X}[\{a^{\prime}\}]\cap\Omega\neq\varnothing
.

We have:

\delta_{X}(a^{\prime})=([a^{\prime}]_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}

so if
R=R_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
, then
[a^{\prime}]_{R_{i}}=\mu_{R^{\prime}R_{i}}([a^{\prime}]_{R^{\prime}})=[a_{i}]_{R_{i}}
, hence
\delta_{X}(a^{\prime})\in\Omega
. Thus:

\delta_{X}(a^{\prime})\in([a^{\prime}]_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\delta_{X}[\{a^{\prime}\}]\cap\Omega\neq\varnothing.

Claim 3:

\delta_{X}:X\to X_{\infty}
is surjective.

Since
(X,\tau)
is a compact space,
\delta_{X}:X\to X_{\infty}
is continuous and
X_{\infty}
is a Hausdorff space, it follows that
\delta_{X}
is a closed map. Hence,

\delta_{X}[X]={\rm Cl}.(\delta_{X}[X])=X_{\infty}.

Thus
\delta_{X}:X\to X_{\infty}
is a continuous closed bijection, hence a homeomorphism.

Let
{\rm\bf BoolSp}
be the category whose objects are all the Boolean spaces and whose morphisms are all the Boolean spaces homomorphisms. Given any Boolean space
(X,\tau)
, let

\mathcal{R}_{X}=\{R\subseteq X\times X|R\mbox{is \,\,\, an \,\,\, equivalence \,\,\, relation\,\,\, on}\,\,X\,\,\mbox{and}\\
\frac{X}{R}\,\,\,\mbox{is \,\,\, discrete \,\,\, and \,\,\, compact}\}

We are going to describe an equivalence functor between the category of the Boolean spaces and the category of profinite spaces.

First we note that given any continuous function
f:X\to X^{\prime}
and any
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}
,

R_{f}:=(f\times f)^{\dashv}[R^{\prime}]\subseteq X\times X

is an equivalence relation on
X
and the following diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.29085pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.72653pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.19968pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.35693pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p_{R_{f}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.68451pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.13716pt\raise-16.00891pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.21725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p_{R}^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.13716pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{R_{f}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.71082pt\raise-24.32062pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.1639pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.96182pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.96182pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\dfrac{X^{\prime}}{R^{\prime}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
p_{R_{f}}:X\to\dfrac{X}{R_{f}}
and
p_{R^{\prime}}:X^{\prime}\to\dfrac{X^{\prime}}{R^{\prime}}
are the canonical projections, commutes.

We know, by
Theorem 4.3
of
[
13
]
that
f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}:\frac{X}{R_{f}}\to\frac{X^{\prime}}{R^{\prime}}
is a continuous map, and it is easy to see that
f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}
is injective, as we are going to show.

Given any
[x]_{R_{f}},[y]_{R_{f}}\in\dfrac{X}{R_{f}}
such that
[x]_{R_{f}}\neq[y]_{R_{f}}
,
i.e.
, such that
(x,y)\notin R_{f}
, we have
(f(x),f(y))\notin R^{\prime}
,
i.e.
,
[f(x)]_{R^{\prime}}\neq[f(y)]_{R^{\prime}}
. Thus, since:

f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}([x]_{R_{f}})=(f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}\circ p_{R_{f}})(x)=(p_{R^{\prime}}\circ f)(x)=[f(x)]_{R^{\prime}}

and

f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}([y]_{R_{f}})=(f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}\circ p_{R_{f}})(y)=(p_{R^{\prime}}\circ f)(y)=[f(y)]_{R^{\prime}}

it follows that
f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}([x]_{R_{f}})\neq f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}([y]_{R_{f}})
.

Since
f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}:\frac{X}{R_{f}}\to\frac{X^{\prime}}{R^{\prime}}
is an injective continuous map and
\frac{X^{\prime}}{R^{\prime}}
is discrete, it follows that given any
[x^{\prime}]_{R^{\prime}}\in\frac{X^{\prime}}{R^{\prime}}
:

f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}^{\dashv}[\{[x^{\prime}]_{R^{\prime}}\}]=\begin{cases}\varnothing,\,\,\mbox{if}\,\,[x^{\prime}]_{R^{\prime}}\in{\rm im}\,(f_{R_{f}R^{\prime}}),\\
\{*\},\,\,\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases},

so every singleton of
\frac{X}{R_{f}}
is an open subset of
\frac{X}{R_{f}}
, and
\frac{X}{R_{f}}
is discrete. Also, since
X
is compact,
\frac{X}{R_{f}}
is compact, and it follows that and
R_{f}\in\mathcal{R}_{X}
.

Now, if
R_{1}^{\prime},R_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}
are such that
R_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq R_{2}^{\prime}
, then
{R_{1}^{\prime}}_{f}\subseteq{R_{2}^{\prime}}_{f}
. In fact, given
(x,y)\in{R_{1}^{\prime}}_{f}
, we have
(f\times f)(x,y)\in R_{1}^{\prime}
, and since
R_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq R_{2}^{\prime}
, it follows that
(f\times f)(x,y)\in R_{2}^{\prime}
, so
(x,y)\in{R_{2}^{\prime}}_{f}
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be Boolean spaces,
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}Y
and
Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}Z
be two Boolean spaces homomorphisms and
R\in\mathcal{R}_{Z}
. We have:

((g\circ f)\times(g\circ f))^{\dashv}[R]=(f\times f)^{\dashv}[(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]].

In fact,

(x,x^{\prime})\in((g\circ f)\times(g\circ f))^{\dashv}[R]\iff(g(f(x)),g(f(x^{\prime})))\in R\iff\\
\iff(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\in(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]\iff(x,x^{\prime})\in(f\times f)^{\dashv}[(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]].

Denoting
T:=(f\times f)^{\dashv}[(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]]
and
S:=(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]
, we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{T}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{S}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{R}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g\circ f)_{TR}}
\scriptstyle{f_{TS}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{Y}{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{SR}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{Z}{R}}

Given a continuous map between Boolean spaces,
f:X\to X^{\prime}
, we can define a map
\check{f}:\varprojlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{X}}\dfrac{X}{R}\to\varprojlim_{R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}}\dfrac{X^{\prime}}{R^{\prime}}
in a functorial manner.

Let
R^{\prime},S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}
be any two equivalence relations such that
R^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
, so given
f:X\to X^{\prime}
the following rectangle commutes:

\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{S_{f}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{f}^{\prime}S_{f}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{S_{f}^{\prime}S^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X^{\prime}}{R_{f}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{R_{f}^{\prime}R^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X^{\prime}}{S^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R^{\prime}S^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X^{\prime}}{R^{\prime}}}

and since:

\textstyle{X_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{S_{f}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{f}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{S_{f}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R_{f}^{\prime}S_{f}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\dfrac{X}{R_{f}^{\prime}}}

commutes, the following triangle is commutative:

, there is a unique

is a functor.

Let

be such that

. Since for any

, then the following diagram commutes for every

Let
X,Y,Z
be Boolean spaces,
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}Y
and
Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}Z
be two Boolean spaces homomorphisms and
R\in\mathcal{R}_{Z}
. We have:

((g\circ f)\times(g\circ f))^{\dashv}[R]=(f\times f)^{\dashv}[(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]].

In fact,

(x,x^{\prime})\in((g\circ f)\times(g\circ f))^{\dashv}[R]\iff(g(f(x)),g(f(x^{\prime})))\in R\iff\\
\iff(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\in(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]\iff(x,x^{\prime})\in(f\times f)^{\dashv}[(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]].

T:=(f\times f)^{\dashv}[(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]]
and
S:=(g\times g)^{\dashv}[R]

is a functor.

Theorem 3.3

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
field.
Following the notation of
Lemma
6
, define the
contravariant functor:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\widehat{k}:&{\rm\bf BoolSp}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}\\
&(X,\tau)&\mapsto&R_{X}:=\varinjlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\mathbb{K}^{U\left(\frac{X}{R}\right)}\end{array}

Then there is a natural isomorphism

\epsilon:Id_{\rm\bf BoolSp}\overset{\cong}{\to}{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\circ\widehat{k}.

Thus the functor
{\rm Spec}^{\infty}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}\rightarrow{\rm\bf BoolSp}
is full and

isomorphism-dense
.

In particular: for each
(X,\tau)
be a Boolean space, there is a von Neumann-regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring,
R_{X}
, such that:

{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(R_{X})\approx X.

.

Proof

By the
Theorem 34
, p. 118 of
[
6
]
,

{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(R_{X})\approx\varprojlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}}{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(\mathbb{K}^{U\left(\frac{X}{R}\right)}).

By the
Theorem 33
, p. 118 of
[
6
]
,

{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(\mathbb{K}^{U\left(\frac{X}{R}\right)})\approx{\rm Discr}.\,\left(\frac{X}{R}\right),

so

\varprojlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}}{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(\mathbb{K}^{U\left(\frac{X}{R}\right)})\approx\varprojlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}}{\rm Discr.}\,\left(\frac{X}{R}\right)\approx\varprojlim_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{X}{R}\approx X

Since the homeomorphisms above are natural, just take
\epsilon_{X}:X\to{\rm Spec}^{\infty}(R_{X})
as the composition of the these homeomorphisms.

Hence, in particular,
{\rm Spec}^{\infty}\,(R_{X})\approx X
.

Theorem 3.4

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
field.
Define the covariant functor (composition of contravariant functors):

\check{K}=\widehat{k}\circ{\rm Stone}:{\rm\bf Bool}\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}.

Then there is a natural isomorphism

\theta:{\rm Id}_{\rm\bf Bool}\overset{\cong}{\to}\to\widetilde{B}\circ\check{K}.

Thus the functor
\widetilde{B}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}{\rm\bf vNRng}\rightarrow{\rm\bf Bool}
is full and

isomorphism-dense
.

In particular: given any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
field
\mathbb{K}
and any
Boolean algebra
B
, there is a von
Neumann regular
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}-
ring which is a
\mathbb{K}-
algebra,

\check{K}(B))
, such that

\widetilde{B}(\check{K}(B))\cong B

.

Proof

This follows directly consequence by a combination of
the
Theorem
3.3
above,
Stone duality (
Remark
6
),
Theorem

3.2
and
Theorem
3.1
.

The diagram below summarizes the main functorial connections
stablished in this section:
